| 887 | 20260816120000 | **LOCAL CANDIDATE — NOT APPLIED.** File: `20260816120000_draw_down_split_order_lines_by_price_tier.sql`, branch `claude/draw-down-price-tier-lines`. Drawing down a booking that lists the same product at two different prices used to collapse both onto one order line priced at the weighted average — a value that is generally not a whole number of cents. Today that draw simply fails: the whole-cent CHECK on `order_items.price_per_unit` and the below-cost trigger both reject a fractional unit price, so the order is never created. Rounding that average is worse than the failure, because the rounded per-unit figure is then multiplied by quantity, so the error scales with the order size rather than staying at a cent; an earlier candidate took that route and was blocked by the adversarial review gate and abandoned. This migration removes the average instead: `CREATE OR REPLACE` on `public._draw_down_quote_below_cost_impl_20260810(uuid, jsonb, uuid, text)` emits one `order_items` row per distinct `(price_per_unit, cost_at_quote_cents)` tier, each carrying the quote's own price — already guaranteed whole cents by `quote_items_price_per_unit_cent_scale_chk` — so no unit price is ever rounded and money is exact by construction. Attribution is derived from the order lines that still bill the customer, not from a running total. An earlier draft walked a cursor down the tier list by how many units had been drawn so far; that is unsound, because those totals move backwards — voiding an order subtracts from them — so a tier already sold could be sold a second time. Instead, each surviving draw line already carries the tier key it was billed at, so a tier is consumed exactly to the extent that live lines were billed at it. The two reversal states are treated differently, matching what each actually reverses: a voided order's lines drop out entirely and its tier returns to the pool, while a cancelled order keeps its delivered units and keeps holding their tier. Job reservations and any legacy averaged lines carry no matching tier key and are taken off the front of the list. Tier order follows the customer's document order — section position, then line position within the section — rather than line position alone, which restarts per section. A fail-closed `DRAW_ALLOCATION_MISMATCH` assertion refuses the whole draw if the split ever fails to conserve quantity. Acres are prorated with the residual assigned to each product's last line. The same round-then-multiply defect on the **cost** side is removed too: each tier stamps its own exact `cost_at_quote_cents` instead of a rounded weighted-average cost. Inventory prebooking, the `inventory_transactions` entry, hold consumption and the `quote_product_draws` ledger stay per product and still move the full drawn quantity exactly once; the returned JSON keeps its per-product shape, so no frontend change is required. Read-only preflight pins the live body at md5 `87bf7adcdc63d94684676da5ab09bfde` and fails closed on drift; postflight re-asserts `SECURITY DEFINER`, the pinned `search_path`, absence of the abandoned averaging candidate's identifier — a name tripwire against a straight revert, not a behaviour check — presence of the conservation assertion, that the implementation stays unreachable from `anon`/`authenticated`/`service_role`/`public`, and — the mirror of that check — that `postgres` still holds EXECUTE, so the defensive revoke cannot silently break the wrapper that reaches it, and that the public wrapper itself is the only function of its name, still carries its five-argument signature, is still `SECURITY DEFINER`, still carries its pinned search path, still calls the below-cost approval gate, and is **not** executable by `anon` — the wrapper is the front door, so its grants are asserted rather than assumed. Known behaviour change: below-cost detection becomes per tier, so a draw whose average was above cost but which contains a genuinely below-cost tier will now correctly require admin below-cost approval. Verified read-only against production on 2026-08-16 that no quote currently lists a product at more than one price and that no job reservations exist, so applying this rewrites no existing order and changes no figure already on the books; it only changes how future multi-tier bookings are drawn. **Known limit, disclosed rather than fixed:** the tier key is not immutable. The below-cost approval trigger on `order_items` fires on insert and on updates to product, price or quantity, and when the declared operation is one of the four ordinary order-editing operations it overwrites that line's stored cost snapshot with today's catalog cost — a price edit rewrites both halves of the key at once. A later edit to a draw-created order can therefore de-link that line from its tier. The failure is fail-closed: the tiers stop summing to the drawn quantity and the conservation assertion refuses the next draw rather than mis-pricing it, so nothing is billed wrong, but a legitimate later draw would be refused until an admin looks. The durable fix is to key on the immutable `order_items.quote_item_id` — the column exists and this path leaves it NULL today — and is tracked as follow-up work, deliberately not attempted here because populating it changes what every downstream consumer of order lines sees. Re-verified read-only against production on 2026-08-16 that no booking-draw order line bills more units than its tier was booked for, and that none matches no tier at all. Reviewed on 2026-08-16 by both gate reviewers: zero BLOCKERs and zero HIGH from the RLS/security pass, zero BLOCKERs from the drift pass, and every actionable finding from both cleared in-file. That pass ran against the text as it stood before the tier-ordering P1 fix recorded below; both gate reviewers must be re-run against the current text before any live apply is considered. Verified read-only against production the same day that the preflight md5 matches the live body exactly, that exactly one wrapper overload exists, that it is `SECURITY DEFINER` with the pinned search path, that `anon` cannot execute it while `authenticated` can, and that the `quote_items.section_id → quote_sections.id` foreign key the tier partition relies on is present. Re-proven end to end on fresh throwaway databases: ten scenarios covering multi-tier draws, split draws, acre proration, void, cancel-with-partial-delivery, job reservations, section ordering and three refusal paths, all exact with zero errors. Typecheck, lint and the full 4,565-test suite pass. **One P1 found in adversarial review after that proof run and fixed:** the tier's document position was originally taken as two independent minima — the lowest section position and the lowest line position, computed separately — which for a tier appearing in two sections can name a position no quote line actually occupies (a tier at section 1/line 10 and section 2/line 1 reported 1/1) and therefore sort ahead of a tier that genuinely comes first, billing the wrong price on a partial draw. Both values are now taken from the same first row under one lexicographic ordering, so the pair is always a real position. Proven rather than argued: a new scenario places one tier in two sections and draws part of the booking, and the same fixture was run twice on fresh throwaway databases — once against the pre-fix body, which billed the wrong tier, and once against the fixed body, which bills the tier the customer's document lists first, conserves quantity across both draws and lands the revenue exactly. All eleven earlier scenarios were re-run against the fixed body on fresh databases with zero errors and no failed assertion. **A second adversarial pass then found one HIGH, also fixed:** the quote lock selected on id alone, and because a quote is soft-deleted by stamping `deleted_at` while its status is left untouched, a deleted booking still read as `sent`, passed the `BOOKING_CLOSED` guard and stayed drawable by anyone holding its id — minting order lines, commissions, inventory reservations and ledger rows against a booking the business considers gone. The lock now requires `deleted_at IS NULL`. This was a pre-existing hole carried unchanged from `20260702172000`, not a regression introduced here; it is closed in this migration because this migration rewrites that body. Proven in both directions on fresh throwaway databases: two identical `sent` bookings, one live and one soft-deleted, run against the pre-fix body — which drew the deleted booking — and against the fixed body, which refuses it, writes no order, no draw ledger row and no inventory movement, while the live booking still draws and bills exactly. The same reviewer's non-blocking note is also addressed: because `draw_down_quote` is marked delegated, the generic idempotency body scan skips it, so its key forwarding rested on prose alone; a focused test now asserts the whole chain, and both new tests were mutation-checked — each goes red against the corresponding broken text. Cross-representative access is deliberately unchanged: any active admin or sales rep may draw any booking, re-confirmed by the owner on 2026-08-16 and recorded in the SQL so later reviewers do not re-raise it. All prior scenarios re-run clean against this body. **A third adversarial pass found one more HIGH, CRX-MONEY-001, closed by an apply-time guard, and a fourth pass then widened that guard.** The new body takes any already-billed line matching no price tier off the FRONT of the tier list. Such a line can only come from a draw made before this migration, when the old code billed one quantity-weighted average. Front-of-list conserves quantity but not money: the average consumed the tiers proportionally, so retiring the cheapest units first leaves the dearer tiers to bill and the booking bills out above its own value — a 100-at-$1.00 plus 100-at-$2.00 booking, partially drawn at the $1.50 average, would bill $325.00 against a $300.00 booking, and `DRAW_ALLOCATION_MISMATCH` would not catch it because it proves quantity was allocated, not that the right monetary tiers were consumed. The first guard tested whether a billed line matched no tier, and the fourth adversarial pass showed that test is too narrow: a weighted average can land exactly on a real tier — 100 each at $1.00, $2.00 and $3.00 averages to $2.00 — and then the tier-matching join subtracts the whole legacy quantity from a tier that cannot hold it, the clamp discards the excess, and the booking bills out *below* its value. The test that would catch the first case passes precisely when the second one happens. The guard is therefore no longer a price test at all: it refuses to apply if any still-drawable booking carries more than one price tier for a product **and** that pair was already drawn under the weighted-average code, whatever it was billed at, because the average does not carry the information needed to recover which tiers those units consumed. Evidence of a prior draw is read from either side — a surviving booking-draw order line or a `quote_product_draws` ledger row — so a voided order still counts. The tier key is the `(price_per_unit, cost_at_quote_cents)` pair, so two lines at the same price with different snapshot costs are two tiers. It refuses with `DRAW_DOWN_PREMIGRATION_MIXED_TIER_DRAW`. Inferring the historical allocation was considered and deliberately rejected, as was a proportional skip: the first would write a guess into customer money, and the second would add new integer-allocation math to a path that holds zero rows now and can hold none after apply, since every draw from here on writes tier-keyed lines that match. Measured read-only against live on 2026-08-15 and again on 2026-08-16, no quote carries more than one price tier for the same product, so nothing is affected today — but a measurement is not an apply-time guarantee, and the current live code still accepts a mixed-price booking whenever its average lands on a whole cent, so one could be created and partially drawn between review and apply. Proven in both directions on a throwaway PostgreSQL 16.14 database with the guard text extracted verbatim from the migration, across seven scenarios: it raised and counted exactly 4 — the average matching no tier, the average landing on a real tier, the coincidence case recorded only in the draw ledger behind a voided order, and two lines at one price with differing costs — while three controls contributed nothing, namely a single-tier booking drawn at an odd price, a soft-deleted mixed-tier booking, and, most importantly, an ordinary mixed-tier booking with no draw yet, which is exactly the case this migration exists to serve and which the guard must never block. With only the four hazards’ draw evidence removed and all three controls intact, the same block completed silently. **A fourth adversarial pass found one more HIGH, CRX-MONEY-002, and it is closed in two halves.** `quote_items.total_units_needed` is `numeric` with no CHECK, so a negative or non-finite booked quantity is representable. The per-product balance sums every one of the product’s quote lines, negatives included, while the tier pool takes only lines above zero — the averaging code this replaces read one weighted price over the same negative-inclusive set, so a negative line pulled the billed price down, and the split cannot do that because a negative line has no tier to be billed at. A booking of 100 units at $2.00 and −50 units at $4.00 is worth $0.00 and still reports 50 units drawable: the old code billed those 50 at the $0.00 average, the split would take all 50 from the $2.00 tier and invoice $100.00 against a booking worth nothing. The conservation assertion cannot catch it, because excluding the negative line makes the tier pool larger than the balance, never smaller, and that assertion fires only when the pool is too small. The function body now refuses such a draw with `BOOKING_QUANTITY_INVALID` before writing anything, and the migration additionally adds a validated CHECK, `quote_items_total_units_needed_nonneg_finite_chk`, so the value cannot be written at all; the body still refuses rather than trusting the constraint, because a constraint can be dropped and this path prices customer money. The finiteness half is not decoration: PostgreSQL orders NaN above every number, so a sign test alone would admit NaN. Verified read-only against production on 2026-08-16 that the column carries no CHECK today and that no row holds a negative or non-finite quantity, so the constraint is added validated rather than NOT VALID, and no existing row is rejected by it. Postflight asserts the constraint is present, validated, and carries both halves of its definition. Proven on fresh throwaway PostgreSQL 16.14 databases: the constraint rejects −1, NaN, Infinity and −Infinity by name while accepting NULL, zero and a positive quantity; the function body, on a database with the constraint deliberately absent, refuses the exploit booking and writes no order and no draw-ledger row, while the same booking with the second line made positive still draws all 150 units and bills exactly $400.00 as two tier lines. Mutation-checked against the pre-fix body on the identical fixture, which drew the exploit booking and billed $100.00 against a booking worth $0.00. Both gate reviewers must be re-run against this text before any live apply, and the schema registry must be refreshed after apply because this migration now adds a constraint as well as replacing a function. **A fifth adversarial pass raised CRX-MIG-002, a medium, and it is closed.** The constraint above was created only when its NAME was free, and the postflight only searched the stored rule for the substrings `>= (0)` and `Infinity`. Under constraint-name drift that pair is not enough: a same-named weaker rule can contain both tokens and still admit the values the constraint exists to stop. Proven on a throwaway PostgreSQL 16.14 database — a same-named rule joining the two halves with OR instead of AND stored −1 successfully, and the old substring postflight passed it. Both halves now compare the stored rule to the exact expression PostgreSQL normalises this CHECK to, captured from `pg_get_constraintdef` rather than hand-written: if the name is already taken by a different rule the migration aborts with `CONSTRAINT_NAME_DRIFT` instead of adopting it unread, and the postflight asserts equality rather than substring presence. Verified read-only against production that the server is PostgreSQL 17.6, that `quote_items.total_units_needed` is a bare `numeric`, and that the constraint name is free — so the normal path is the create path and the recorded expression is the one this server will produce. Re-proven end to end on fresh throwaway databases: on a clean database the block creates the constraint, the postflight passes, and the constraint rejects −1, NaN, Infinity and −Infinity by name while accepting NULL and a positive quantity; on a database carrying the weaker same-named rule the block aborts. **The same pass raised CRX-MIG-001, a high, and it was a stale branch base rather than an edit:** this branch had never touched `.claude/schema-registry.json`, but it was cut before #406 refreshed that file from live introspection, so merging would have rolled the registry back a day and dropped an applied security migration from its list. Closed by merging `origin/main` into this branch; the registry is now main’s. Both gate reviewers must be re-run against this text before any live apply, and the schema registry must be refreshed after apply because this migration now adds a constraint as well as replacing a function. **A sixth adversarial pass found one more HIGH, and it is closed in two halves.** The mixed-tier apply-time guard above scanned only bookings in `sent` or `revised` status — the two statuses a draw is allowed from — on the reasoning that a booking which cannot be drawn today cannot be mispriced by this path. That reasoning is wrong, because “drawable” is not a stable property of a booking. Drawing one to completion flips it to `accepted`, and every void/cancel reversal path flips `accepted` back to `sent` as soon as a partial cancel, cancel or void leaves it no longer fully drawn (`20260610185806`, `20260613191323`, `20260616142001`, `20260620130000`, `20260721014858`). A booking drawn to completion under the averaging code would therefore have passed the guard, reopened later, and had its remainder allocated with no record of which tiers the average consumed — the same misbill as before, just deferred: 100 units at $1.00 plus 100 at $2.00 drawn at the $1.50 average, then 50 delivered and the remainder cancelled, bills $325.00 against a $300.00 booking, and quantity still conserves so `DRAW_ALLOCATION_MISMATCH` stays silent. The status filter is therefore removed: the guard now keys on the stable predicate — this product on this booking carries more than one tier **and** already carries draw evidence — accepting that a genuinely finished booking will be refused too, a false refusal a human clears once, which is the fail-closed direction and the correct trade against writing a guess into customer money. Proven on a throwaway PostgreSQL 16.14 database by running both scan texts against one fixture: the old scan refused only the `sent` booking and let the `accepted` one through exactly as reported, the new scan refuses both, and neither refuses either control — a single-tier drawn booking and a mixed-tier booking with no draw. The same pass also found the cutover unserialised: the scan took no lock against the old draw body, so a draw that started before the scan could commit after it and be missed, defeating the apply-time guarantee. The migration now takes `SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE` on `quote_product_draws` — the table every draw path writes — before the preflight, held for the applier’s single transaction, so a concurrent legacy draw either commits before the lock and is seen by the scan or blocks and resumes against the new body. Proven on the same database: while that lock is held a concurrent INSERT and a concurrent UPDATE both fail with `canceling statement due to lock timeout`, a plain SELECT is unaffected, and a draw succeeds again once the lock is released. The top-level lock form is confirmed legal for this applier by three migrations already applied live that use it (`20260702135000`, `20260716233000`, `20260717101619`), and the amended file was run in a single transaction to confirm the lock executes and the preflight is reached. Both gate reviewers must be re-run against this text before any live apply. **A seventh adversarial pass returned three more HIGHs, all confirmed against the live database before any change was made, and all now closed.** (1) *An edited tier key re-sells its own units.* Billed lines whose (price, cost) pair matches no quote tier were counted and consumed off the FRONT of the tier list. That is sound for lines the old averaging code wrote, but not for a line whose key was rewritten after it was billed — the tier it was really drawn from stays in the pool, and both get sold. Reachable: the live `order_items` UPDATE policy is `USING/WITH CHECK is_admin()` with no column restriction, and no trigger on that table pins `price_per_unit` or `cost_at_time_cents` (both verified read-only 2026-08-16). Book 100 units at $1.00 and 100 at $2.00, draw the $1.00 tier plus half the $2.00 tier, void the $1.00 order so its tier returns, edit the surviving line’s cost snapshot, and draw the rest: the booking bills $350.00 against $300.00. `DRAW_ALLOCATION_MISMATCH` cannot see it, because that assertion fires only when the pool is too SMALL to absorb the draw, never when it is too large. Closed with a runtime guard, `DRAW_MIXED_TIER_UNMATCHED_LINE`: on a booking carrying more than one tier for the product, any unmatched billed quantity now refuses the draw outright. Single-tier bookings keep the front-walk, where there is nowhere else the units could have come from. (2) *Soft deletion is not a scope, for the same reason status was not.* The apply-time scan still carried `q.deleted_at IS NULL`, but `quotes.deleted_at` can be set straight back to NULL by any admin or by the owning sales rep under the live `quotes_update` policy, with no trigger in the way (verified read-only 2026-08-16). A soft-deleted mixed-tier booking with legacy draw evidence could therefore be restored after this migration shipped. The filter is removed; the scan now covers deleted quotes too. (3) *The cutover lock cannot drain a draw that is already running.* `SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE` blocks an old invocation only when it reaches its ledger write, and that invocation keeps executing the old function body out of its own plan cache after the replacement commits, with its uncommitted rows invisible to the scan. The previous entry’s claim that such a draw “resumes against the new body” was wrong and has been corrected in the file. Eliminating the window needs a barrier inside the OLD body, which can only ship in an earlier, separate migration. What ships here is the net: a late legacy averaged draw leaves exactly the unmatched billed line that guard (1) refuses, so the next draw on that booking stops loudly instead of quietly misbilling the remainder. The window is narrow and no longer silent; it is not closed, and the file no longer claims otherwise. Proven on a throwaway PostgreSQL 16.14 database, with both predicates lifted verbatim out of the migration so the test cannot drift from it. Runtime guard: the reported scenario refuses (2 tiers, 50 unmatched units); a single-tier booking with an orphaned line is allowed; a mixed-tier booking mid-partial-draw with every line matching is allowed; a mixed-tier booking with no draw yet — the case this migration exists to serve — is allowed. Apply-time scan: the old text refused 2 pairs, the new text refuses 3, the extra one being the soft-deleted booking, and neither refuses the never-drawn control. The amended file was then run statement by statement with zero syntax errors, and in a single transaction to confirm the lock executes and the preflight is reached; the replacement body compiles with the new guard present in `prosrc`. Both gate reviewers must be re-run against this text before any live apply. **An eighth adversarial pass returned two more HIGHs, both confirmed against real source before any change was made, and both now closed by one guard.** (1) *A coincidental average defeats the seventh pass's unmatched-line net.* That guard refuses a further draw when a billed line matches no price tier, which is the trace a legacy weighted-average draw normally leaves. But an average can land exactly on a real tier key — 100 units each at $1.00, $2.00 and $3.00 averages to $2.00 — and then the line matches, the guard stays silent, and a late legacy draw of 250 units billed at the coincidental $2.00 lets the next 50-unit draw close a $600.00 booking at $550.00. (2) *A supported quote revision silently overconsumes a matching tier.* This one needs no admin hand-edit and no race. `save_quote`'s drawn-product guard (`20260812115236`, read and confirmed rather than assumed) is aggregate-only: it sums draw-ledger rows by `product_id`, sums `quote_items.total_units_needed` by `product_id`, and refuses only when total booked is less than total drawn. It preserves no per-tier attribution. So book 200 units at $2.00, draw 150 of them, then revise the booking to 100 units at $1.00 plus 100 at $2.00. Booked still covers drawn, so the revision saves. The 150 billed units still match the $2.00 tier, which now holds only 100 — and the per-tier `GREATEST(..., 0)` clamp silently discarded that 50-unit overhang, so the $1.00 tier still read as fully available and the remaining 50 units billed against it: $350.00 against a revised $300.00 booking. Neither existing refusal could fire — the balance guard works on per-product aggregates, and `DRAW_ALLOCATION_MISMATCH` fires only when the tier pool is too SMALL, never when it is too large. The previous entry's assertion that the clamp was "bounded on both sides" by those two was therefore false, and the file's own comment saying so has been corrected rather than quietly dropped. Closed with a second runtime layer, `DRAW_TIER_OVERCONSUMED`: the quantity the clamp used to discard is now carried out of the tier query as its own column, accumulated across every tier (the loop no longer exits at the allocation boundary, so tiers past that point are still inspected), and any nonzero total refuses the draw outright. It catches both findings — the coincidental average is attributed wholly to the one tier it happens to match while it really consumed several, so it over-bills that tier and stops here. The durable fix for both remains immutable tier provenance on the order line, which is a schema change deliberately out of scope for this migration; this refusal is the fail-closed stand-in and is deliberately stricter than strictly necessary. Postflight now also asserts both new guard identifiers are present in the shipped body, alongside the existing conservation-assertion tripwire; each of the three was mutation-checked and goes red when its guard is absent. Proven on a throwaway PostgreSQL 17.10 database with the whole tier/billed query lifted verbatim out of the migration so the test cannot drift from it, across seven scenarios: the revised-booking case refuses with zero unmatched units — proving the seventh-pass guard genuinely would not have caught it — the coincidental-average case refuses likewise, and five controls are all still allowed, namely an ordinary partial draw, a fully drawn booking, a single-tier booking with an orphaned legacy line, a booking whose over-billing sits behind a voided order, and one behind a cancelled order with partial delivery. The amended file was then run statement by statement with zero syntax errors and the replacement body compiles with both new guards present in `prosrc`. **A documentation defect was also corrected in this pass:** five earlier entries above described their throwaway proof server as PostgreSQL 17 when it was in fact PostgreSQL 16.14, and they now say so. The two claims in that set that actually depend on the server version were re-proven on a real PostgreSQL 17.10 throwaway: the CHECK constraint normalises to exactly the expression the migration compares against — byte-identical on both 16.14 and 17.10, so the recorded expression was right and only its provenance was misstated — and the constraint still rejects a negative quantity, NaN, Infinity and −Infinity by name while accepting NULL, zero and a positive quantity. Live remains PostgreSQL 17.6. Both gate reviewers must be re-run against this text before any live apply. **A ninth adversarial pass returned one more HIGH, CRX-MONEY-CUTOVER-001, and closing it properly required a second migration, so this one no longer ships alone.** The finding is a smaller variant of the hole the eighth pass closed, and it survives both runtime nets. `DRAW_TIER_OVERCONSUMED` fires only when billed units exceed the tier they match, and `DRAW_MIXED_TIER_UNMATCHED_LINE` fires only when some billed quantity matches no tier at all. Book 100 units each at $1.00, $2.00 and $3.00 with equal cost and the weighted average is exactly $2.00; an in-flight legacy draw of 50 units then writes a 50-unit line at $2.00 that matches the real $2.00 tier and fits comfortably inside its 100-unit capacity. Nothing is unmatched, nothing is overconsumed, both guards stay silent, and those 50 units should have billed at $1.00 — the order, the profit, the commission basis and the audit amount are each wrong by $50.00. The reviewer was right that a runtime net is the wrong instrument here and that the cutover needs a deterministic barrier rather than an operational "apply when no draws are in flight" instruction. **The reviewer's own prescribed remedy was then tested and found unsafe, and the design changed because of the measurement rather than the argument.** The prescription was to have the draw entry point take an advisory lock and let this migration take the same lock exclusively, so draws WAIT. Measured on a throwaway PostgreSQL 17.10 database, that is wrong and silently so: a session whose plan cache already held the pre-cutover implementation, blocking inside a second call for a measured 4.0 seconds spanning the replacement, resumed and still returned the OLD result, because a backend does not re-check plan invalidations in the middle of an already-started command. A waiting barrier would have converted a rare race into a reliable one. What ships instead is a fail-fast barrier in a separate precursor migration, `20260816110000`, recorded in its own row below and applied FIRST. Draws call `pg_try_advisory_xact_lock_shared(20260816, 1)`, which returns immediately: with the key free the draw proceeds exactly as today holding it in SHARE mode, and with the key held exclusively the draw raises `DRAW_DOWN_CUTOVER_IN_PROGRESS` as its very first act and rolls back having written nothing. This migration now takes the same key with `pg_advisory_xact_lock` before its table lock, which yields three exhaustive cases: a draw that took SHARE first holds it to commit, so this migration blocks and the preflight scan sees that draw's rows; a draw arriving while this migration holds the key exclusively is refused and records nothing; and a draw arriving after commit finds the key free and is planned fresh, so it runs the new body. **There is no interval in which the old body can commit a row this scan did not see, so the window the seventh and eighth passes could only narrow is now eliminated, and the file's cutover comment has been rewritten to say so instead of calling it an operational choice.** The lock ordering is advisory-key-then-table on both sides, so no deadlock is possible between a draw and this cutover. The barrier is not merely assumed to be live: the preflight here refuses to run unless the live wrapper actually carries it, asserting the wrapper is the only function of its name, that both the try-lock call and the refusal identifier are present in its body — a lock with no refusal is the broken waiting form — and that the barrier precedes the forwarded implementation call, since a barrier taken after the call is not a barrier. It aborts with `DRAW_DOWN_CUTOVER_BARRIER_MISSING`, `DRAW_DOWN_CUTOVER_BARRIER_MISORDERED` or `DRAW_DOWN_WRAPPER_OVERLOADED`, so the two migrations cannot be applied out of order and this one cannot be applied alone. That is the durable proof gate the reviewer asked for. Proven on a throwaway PostgreSQL 17.10 database rather than argued. The barrier gate here was lifted verbatim out of this file and run against four wrapper variants: it passes only against the correctly barriered wrapper, and refuses a wrapper with no barrier, a wrapper carrying the waiting form with no refusal, a wrapper whose barrier sits after the forwarded call, and a second unbarriered overload. The whole file was then split into its eight statements and each parsed individually with zero syntax errors — the replacement implementation body compiles in full — and the new advisory-lock statement was confirmed to execute. **Both gate reviewers must be re-run against this text, and the pair must be applied in order — `20260816110000` first, then this one — before any live apply.** **Tenth pass (RLS/security + drift review, 2026-08-16).** The security reviewer found that the barrier binds every draw *planned* after the precursor commits but cannot bind a backend already executing the pre-barrier body at that moment: `CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION` neither interrupts nor drains a running call, so that backend holds no advisory key, and if it has not yet reached its draw-ledger insert the `SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE` lock finds nothing to conflict with and the preflight scan cannot see its uncommitted rows. The fix is a refusal, not an instruction: the preflight now aborts with `DRAW_DOWN_CUTOVER_NOT_QUIET` if ANY other client-backend transaction older than its own is open — a strict superset of the dangerous set, since this transaction necessarily begins after the precursor commits. Blindness is refused first: a role that cannot read other backends' transaction start times would count zero for the wrong reason, so the gate aborts with `DRAW_DOWN_CUTOVER_STATS_BLIND` unless the applying role is superuser or holds `pg_read_all_stats`. **Operator consequences: the apply needs a genuinely quiet database — expect repeated refusals during business hours — and must run as a role holding `pg_monitor` (confirmed read-only on 2026-08-16 that the applying role does).** `SET LOCAL lock_timeout = 15s` now precedes the lock block, so a blocked apply aborts having changed nothing instead of freezing the quote pages; the header no longer claims the migration has no lock footprint, since validating the new CHECK reads every existing row of the quote-items table under `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE`. The drift review then flagged that bundling both migrations into one transaction would defeat the barrier entirely — it never becomes visible to another session, every concurrent draw runs unbarriered, and the catalog assertions still pass by reading the transaction's own uncommitted catalog — and that only prose forbade it. That is now a guard: the preflight compares `pg_proc.xmin` on the wrapper row against its own transaction id and aborts with `DRAW_DOWN_CUTOVER_BARRIER_UNCOMMITTED`. Proven, not argued, on PostgreSQL 17.10 against a fixture reproducing both live body hashes exactly: applied separately, both migrations succeed with their postflight notices; bundled into one transaction, the apply is refused and rolls back with the wrapper still at its pre-barrier hash. The quiet gate itself was mutation-tested four ways — quiet passes, a concurrent open transaction is refused by name and age, a role without stats visibility is refused, and the same role passes once granted — so it fails closed rather than vacuously. Nine statements now, up from eight. Eleventh pass (RLS/security + drift re-review, 2026-08-16; 0 BLOCKERS from both): the reviewers found no security or schema defect but four *false claims in the header comments*, which on this file are load-bearing because its safety argument is the deliverable. Corrected: `DRAW_TIER_OVERCONSUMED` was described as catching the coincidental-average cutover case and does not — it fires only when the attributed quantity exceeds the matched tier's booked units, so an averaged draw that fits inside that tier passes both runtime nets and is closed by the barrier and the quiet gate alone; the worked example is now in the header. The quiet gate dropped its `xact_start <= now()` filter, which was a superset of the dangerous set only under an unenforced assumption that this transaction began after the barrier committed — it now refuses on any other open client-backend transaction and reports age via `clock_timestamp()`. Two guards added and mutation-tested: `DRAW_DOWN_CUTOVER_PREPARED_XACT` (prepared two-phase transactions never appear in `pg_stat_activity`; with a ghost present the backend scan read zero, confirming the blind spot empirically) and `DRAW_DOWN_CUTOVER_NOT_IN_TRANSACTION`, which asserts `lock_timeout` reads back `15s` so a non-transactional apply cannot silently no-op the whole cutover handshake. The bundling check was changed to `IS NOT FALSE` so a NULL reads as refusal, and its top-level-xid subtransaction gap is now recorded rather than implied. Also disclosed: 15s bounds each lock acquisition (~45s worst case, and the figure is unmeasured), and the draw-refusal window spans the `quote_items` CHECK validation, accepted rather than split into a third ordered migration. Re-proven end to end on PostgreSQL 17.10 against a fixture reproducing both live body md5s exactly: separate committed applies succeed with both postflight notices; a bundled apply is refused and rolls back with the wrapper still at its pre-barrier md5; a concurrent transaction, a prepared transaction, and a mutated `lock_timeout` are each refused by name, and each control run succeeds once the condition is cleared. Twelfth pass (push-proof, 2026-08-16, verdict BLOCKERS — one High): CRX-MONEY-TIER-ROUND-001. Every earlier pass had missed it, because it only shows up on FRACTIONAL quantities. The loop rounded each draw's extension in isolation, so on a booking drawn in several partial pieces the per-draw rounding residual accumulated instead of cancelling; the reported case books $1.00 as half a unit at $0.50 plus half a unit at $1.50 and, drawn as four quarter-unit draws, billed $1.02. Both premises were re-verified against current source before accepting the finding: the draw quantity box and the quote-line quantity boxes are all `step="any"`, so fractional draws are a real production input shape, not hypothetical. Fixed by rounding each tier's RUNNING TOTAL and billing the difference — the line owes `ROUND(price*(prior+take),2) - ROUND(price*prior,2)`, where `prior` is a new tier-CTE column holding the units already billed at that tier key — so successive draws telescope and the lines written against a tier sum to exactly `ROUND(price*units_drawn,2)` no matter how the draw was split. Cost uses the same form. Proven in-engine on PostgreSQL 17.10: the reported case now bills exactly $1.00 against $1.02 before; across twelve tier/draw shapes the new form is exact 12 of 12 where the old was exact 3 of 12 with a worst-case 9-cent error; and on four whole-unit shapes the new form is identical to the old, so the ordinary non-fractional draw is unchanged. The header's blanket "money is exact by construction rather than exact by rounding" claim was overstated for fractional quantities and has been narrowed to the property actually guaranteed, with the earlier wording flagged as a draft error. Both migrations re-applied clean in order on a fresh fixture whose pre-apply body hashes match the live pins exactly. NOT verified at that point: a full end-to-end draw through the live function, because the throwaway fixture carried only 7 of the ~12 tables that function touches — the twelfth-pass proof covered the changed arithmetic and the migration's own guards, not the surrounding call path. Thirteenth pass (push-proof, 2026-08-16, verdict BLOCKERS — two High) closed both that gap and the two new findings. CRX-MONEY-LIFECYCLE-001: telescoping on surviving UNITS is exact going forward but not path-independent through a reversal, because the subtrahend `ROUND(price*prior,2)` is a recomputed figure while the money standing on the surviving lines is whatever those lines were actually written at. Two identical quarter-unit draws at $0.50 write $0.13 and $0.12; voiding the first and redrawing the same quantity then billed $0.24, while voiding the second billed $0.25 — the same surviving quantity, two different bills, decided by which of two identical draws was reversed. Fixed by re-basing on the cents actually billed: the `billed` CTE gained a `money` column summing the surviving lines' own `total_price` (with the same cancelled-order branch the units column uses, so money and units always describe the same rows), and the line owes `GREATEST(ROUND(price*(prior+take),2) - money, 0)`. The `GREATEST` refuses to issue a silent credit — a credit belongs in a credit memo, a negative `total_price` would be refused by the whole-cent CHECKs anyway, and the quantity form of the same condition is already surfaced and refused by `DRAW_TIER_OVERCONSUMED`. Measured in-engine on PostgreSQL 17: path-independent where the units basis was not, exact in 36 of 36 void/redraw cases (worst error 0.00) against 26 of 36 for the units basis and 8 of 36 for the original per-draw form, and identical to the original form on all four whole-unit shapes. CRX-MONEY-PROFIT-001: a regression introduced by the twelfth-pass fix itself. Making the COST side cumulative desynchronised the header from the lines, because the canonical `_round_money_to_whole_cents` trigger (`20260809230500`) always overwrites `order_items.profit` with `total_price - ROUND(cost_per_unit * total_units_needed, 2)` and discards any caller-supplied profit, so a cumulative cost basis reached the header and the commission basis but never the stored lines. Fixed by computing the line cost with the trigger's own per-line expression; header, lines, and commission basis agree exactly (gap 0.01 → 0.00 across eight shapes). The asymmetry is deliberate: revenue telescopes and is exact to the cent, while cost keeps the ordinary per-line residual every other order path already carries — removing it would mean changing the shared trigger for ALL order lines and re-opening a settled 2026-08-09 decision, which is out of scope here. The end-to-end gap is now closed: the fixture was extended with the nine missing tables (`profiles`, `customers`, `job_product_draws`, `inventory`, `inventory_holds`, `inventory_transactions`, `financial_audit_log`, `activity_feed`, `commissions`) plus the canonical profit trigger verbatim, and the REAL `draw_down_quote` was run. That immediately caught a genuine defect no model-level proof could see — the `money` aggregate had been added to the CTE but never projected into the record the tier loop iterates, so the function failed at runtime with `record "v_tier" has no field "money"` — since fixed. Observed end to end: the $1.00 two-tier booking drawn as four quarter-unit draws bills exactly $1.00, and every order's header profit, summed line profit, and commission basis match; the void-and-redraw case bills exactly $0.25 (voided line $0.13, surviving $0.12 + $0.13). Both migrations still apply clean in order on a fresh fixture whose pre-apply body hashes match the live pins exactly. **Fourteenth pass (root-cause rework, 2026-08-19) replaces the tier key itself, so the `(price_per_unit, cost_at_quote_cents)` description at the top of this entry no longer describes the shipped body.** Every defect the eighth through thirteenth passes fenced off with runtime refusals shares one cause: the tier key was neither unique nor immutable. Two booked lines at the same price and cost collapsed into one bucket, and three separate paths rewrite the cost snapshot after a line is billed, so attributing already-billed units to the tier that sold them was a guess the guards could only refuse rather than resolve. A tier is now one booked quote line. The draw stamps `order_items.quote_item_id` on every line it writes, and the next draw reads that column back, so attribution is an identity lookup; the price-and-cost match survives only as the fallback for lines carrying no stamp, which is exactly the pre-migration population. Allocation walks the tiers in the customer's own document order, so an interleaved A/B/A booking bills the B line before it reaches the second A block instead of collapsing both A blocks into one bucket. Acres now come from each line's own booked rate rather than from prorating one whole-draw figure, which on a product booked at two rates invented a third rate that reached the customer through `complete_delivery`. `DRAW_TIER_OVERCONSUMED` and `DRAW_MIXED_TIER_UNMATCHED_LINE` are both kept: with provenance present they should be unreachable, and they remain the net for the residue case where a product's tiers genuinely share one price and cost. **Stamping forced a foreign-key change in the same migration, and that is the part that needed the most care.** `save_quote` rebuilds a quote by deleting its sections; `quote_items_section_id_fkey` is `ON DELETE CASCADE`, so those quote lines go with them. `order_items_quote_item_id_fkey` was `NO ACTION` and not deferrable — both read from the live catalog on 2026-08-19 — which means the first stamped order line would have made its booking permanently un-editable with a raw foreign-key violation, and `save_quote` carries no order-aware guard that would have produced a readable refusal instead. The constraint therefore moves to `ON DELETE NO ACTION DEFERRABLE INITIALLY DEFERRED` here, so the referential check happens at COMMIT rather than at the end of that DELETE. By COMMIT `save_quote` has reinserted the quote's lines under the same ids, so the stamp SURVIVES an ordinary revision and attribution stays exact. **This replaces an earlier `ON DELETE SET NULL` rule that was reviewed and found wrong.** `save_quote` runs its DELETE on every save of an existing quote, including a save that changes nothing, so SET NULL wiped every stamp every time: that resets the telescoping rounding basis, re-opening the fractional overbill this migration exists to close, and strands a partly-drawn two-price booking behind `DRAW_MIXED_TIER_UNMATCHED_LINE`, whose message tells the operator to undo a revision SET NULL has already made impossible to undo. The earlier draft rejected `DEFERRABLE INITIALLY DEFERRED` on the ground that id reuse depended on the client echoing existing line ids back; live `prosrc` read on 2026-08-19 shows that premise is wrong in both halves — no current page echoes ids (`QuoteBuilder.tsx` and `BulkQuoteImport.tsx` both send none), and `save_quote`'s id-less fallback reuses prior ids without them. Deferring keeps the delete rule at NO ACTION, so nothing nulls the stamp and a save that genuinely failed to bring an id back aborts rather than committing an orphan. The constraint is matched **structurally** on catalog columns — `confdeltype`, `confupdtype`, `confmatchtype`, `condeferrable`, `convalidated`, and the single-column key names — rather than on `pg_get_constraintdef` text, because that rendering schema-qualifies the referenced table only when it sits outside the session's current `search_path`, and this file pins `search_path` inside the function body only; a text match would turn a differing apply session into a false `FK_RULE_DRIFT` abort. The exact predicate was run read-only against live: the constraint reads `confdeltype = 'a'` with the expected shape, so the apply takes the drop-and-re-add branch, and it is re-added validated rather than `NOT VALID` because the supporting index already exists and a `NOT VALID` constraint would leave unchecked the very orphans it exists to prevent. The preamble now also locks `quote_items` and `order_items` in `SHARE ROW EXCLUSIVE` alongside the existing `quote_product_draws` lock, acquired in the order the application itself reaches those tables so the cutover contends head-on rather than crossing; `order_items` is taken last because the foreign-key swap must later upgrade it to `ACCESS EXCLUSIVE`, and holding the self-conflicting mode from the start means that upgrade waits only on readers. Postflight gained assertions that the installed body both reads and writes the stamp, that it still partitions a tier's billed history by price (`units_current` / `units_settled`), and that the constraint landed as NO ACTION **and** deferred — the SET NULL rule is explicitly refused rather than adopted. Proven read-only on synthetic `VALUES` rows with no table touched: telescoping across one tier at $1.01 drawn as four quarter-unit draws now totals exactly $1.01 where per-draw rounding totalled $1.00; document-order allocation on an interleaved 100/100/100 booking takes 100, 50 and 0 units and bills $1,600.00 where the collapsed-bucket form billed $1,500.00; two separate half-unit lines at the same $1.01 price bill $1.02 per line against $1.01 as one bucket; and per-line acres come out 100.00, 5.00 and NULL against the prorated 36.67 / 18.33. `npm run typecheck` is clean and the full suite passes at 4,581 tests. The body also now applies Mason's 2026-08-19 pricing rule: each tier's billed history is partitioned by whether the line was billed at the price the quote line carries today, so units billed at a superseded price are **settled** — they consume the tier's capacity but never re-enter the telescoping basis, and new units bill at the new price from a fresh basis. Without that split, raising a price on a partly-drawn booking silently rebilled units already delivered. Proven on a throwaway PostgreSQL 17.6 in Docker (live never written to): the tier query lifted verbatim from this file parses and runs; seven money scenarios pass against expected values, including $130.00 for `40 x $1.00 + 60 x $1.50` after a mid-booking price rise and $350.00 for a two-tier booking with a price change between draws; the pre-rework projection bills $150.00 on that first scenario, so the scenario is mutation-proven rather than vacuous; the real FK `DO` block installs `confdeltype a / condeferrable t / condeferred t`, is idempotent, and refuses a drifted SET NULL rule; and a `save_quote`-shaped delete-and-reinsert preserves the stamp under the new rule, aborts under the old non-deferrable rule, and silently wipes the stamp under the retired SET NULL rule. **Not verified, stated rather than implied:** this file has never been applied end to end by a server, because the preflight requires the cutover barrier committed in a prior transaction. Both gate reviewers must be re-run against this text, and the pair must still be applied in order — `20260816110000` first, then this one. Two pre-existing defects found while verifying this rework are recorded in `docs/manual/KNOWN_ISSUES.md` and deliberately **not** fixed here, because each needs its own decision: `link_blend_ticket_to_order` picks one order line per product with `LIMIT 1`, which has no money impact today only because `quantity_applied` is read by nothing, and `create_invoice_from_blend_ticket` picks its price with `ORDER BY qi.id LIMIT 1`, which on a multi-tier booking is an arbitrary uuid ordering and is a live money defect independent of this migration. SQL sha256: `2d88735e623877b3d63180f35c8d2122fead62409f29b6607b0c883bedf28324`. **This migration now replaces TWO functions, not one (Mason's option A, 2026-08-19).** The RLS gate found that `save_quote` is not the only path that deletes and reinserts a quote's lines: `_restore_quote_version_owner_impl` does the same, and its reinsert omits the `id` column entirely, so every restored line takes a fresh uuid and no id is ever reused. Under the deferred FK that would leave every stamp dangling at COMMIT and abort a version restore of any partially drawn booking with a raw foreign-key error, on a UI-reachable path that works today — a regression the retired SET NULL design did not have. The restore path is therefore replaced in the SAME transaction so the two cannot ship apart, and it now RAISES `QUOTE_RESTORE_BLOCKED_BY_DRAW` — a plain-English refusal — when the booking already has drawn lines, before any destructive work. An earlier draft RELEASED the stamps instead (`SET quote_item_id = NULL`); the `gpt-5.6-sol` gate refuted that on 2026-08-19 and it was reproduced on PostgreSQL 17.6: releasing discards the telescoping rounding basis, so two 0.5-unit lines at $1.01 drawn 0.5 and then restored to a single 1-unit version bill $1.02 against a $1.01 booking, and `DRAW_MIXED_TIER_UNMATCHED_LINE` cannot catch it because that guard fires only above one distinct (price, cost) while the restored quote carries exactly one. The release also fired `after_order_items_change` → `trg_recalc_order_totals` under the quote lock, crossing the order→quote order cancel/void takes. The refusal is deliberately BROAD, and an earlier draft of this row understated it: the check joins `order_items` unfiltered by order status, so once a booking has ever been drawn it can never restore a version again — even after every draw order is cancelled or voided and the booking reopens, because those rows are retained for audit and still carry their stamp. Codex round 3 caught the overstatement; Mason accepted the limitation on 2026-08-20 rather than narrow it, because narrowing requires releasing the stamps on those dead lines and that reintroduces the `order_items` UPDATE whose `after_order_items_change` -> `trg_recalc_order_totals` locks the order row under the quote lock — the deadlock this rework just removed. A regression case in `scripts/smoke/smoke-restore-version-drawn-guard.sql` pins the accepted behaviour. Editing the quote directly still works on any booking, and carrying the billing basis across a restore is deferred to the same PR that owes the `QUOTE_ITEM_AMBIGUOUS_COST` line-identity fix. The same gate also caught a guaranteed-rollback blocker: the postflight denied `service_role` EXECUTE on the restore impl, but live carries `{postgres=X/postgres,service_role=X/postgres}` (a grant `20260813080000` deliberately retained) and `CREATE OR REPLACE` preserves ACLs, so the assertion fired and rolled the whole migration back every time — missed originally because the rehearsal created the function fresh with no inherited ACL, making the check pass vacuously. The deny list is now the browser roles only and `service_role` is asserted present. The body is reproduced byte-exactly from the live definition (md5 `d8408e3b19b536f1210e51da3970272e`) with exactly one statement added; a new preflight pins that md5 (`RESTORE_IMPL_DRIFTED` / `RESTORE_IMPL_OVERLOADED` / `RESTORE_IMPL_MISSING`) and a new postflight asserts the release is present, sits before the delete, and did not weaken the function's SECURITY DEFINER posture, search_path pin or grants. Proven on PostgreSQL 17.6 with a fixture reproducing live's ACL exactly: the old grant assertion trips on `service_role` while the corrected one passes; the shipped body installs over that preimage and the ACL survives `CREATE OR REPLACE`; all four restore postflight predicates hold; the restore writes `order_items` nowhere so the header trigger cannot fire; a drawn booking is refused, an undrawn one is not, the guard is scoped per quote, and the $1.02 overbill is unreachable. Both gate reviewers also found a real lock-order crossing — this file locked `quote_product_draws` before `order_items` while the draw path writes `order_items` first — which is fixed (order is now `quote_items` -> `order_items` -> `quote_product_draws`) along with the header's false "no deadlock is possible" claim and its understated lock count (six acquisitions, ~90s worst case). The FK postflight now also asserts `convalidated` and the full key shape, mutation-proven: a NOT VALID constraint passes the old predicate and is rejected by the new one. **Live apply is not yet approved and has not happened.** |

Waiting would have made the race reliable instead of rare. Failing fast costs a retry; waiting costs money. Draws never conflict with each other, since SHARE does not conflict with SHARE, so ordinary operation is unchanged and there is no new contention between concurrent reps. The key uses the TWO-INTEGER advisory form on purpose: PostgreSQL keeps single-bigint and two-integer advisory locks in separate, non-overlapping lock spaces, and every existing advisory lock in this schema uses the single-bigint `hashtext()` form, so collision is impossible by construction rather than by luck. **Safe on its own:** if the tier-split migration is never applied, nothing ever takes this key exclusively, every try succeeds on the first attempt, and the function behaves exactly as it does today. The `REVOKE`/`GRANT` pair restates the known-good posture from `20260812115237` rather than changing it — `CREATE OR REPLACE` preserves the ACL — and the only live caller, the quote builder's draw modal, is an ordinary authenticated RPC that the REVOKE does not name. Postflight asserts overload uniqueness, `SECURITY DEFINER`, the pinned `search_path`, presence of both the try-lock call and the refusal identifier, that the barrier precedes both the below-cost approval gate and the forwarded implementation call, that the gate and the forwarded `p_idempotency_key` are still there, and that `anon` still cannot execute the wrapper. Proven end to end on a throwaway PostgreSQL 17.10 database, not argued. The fixture reproduced the live wrapper body byte-for-byte — same md5 and same 292-byte length — which independently confirms the pin. The migration then applied cleanly with its postflight notice. Behaviour: an ordinary draw succeeds; two concurrent draws both succeed with no blocking; a draw arriving while the key is held exclusively is refused instantly with the plain-language error and writes nothing; a draw already holding the key made the cutover block for a measured 4.0 seconds until that draw committed, which is exactly the property that lets the tier-split preflight see its rows; and the same session that was refused mid-cutover retried successfully two seconds after the cutover committed. The postflight tripwires were mutation-checked against nine broken variants — barrier removed, barrier taken with no refusal, barrier after the approval gate, barrier after the forwarded call, approval gate removed, idempotency key not forwarded, `search_path` dropped, `anon` granted EXECUTE, and a second overload added — and every one goes red while only the correct body passes. All five statements in the file were parsed individually with zero syntax errors. **Second pass (2026-08-16).** The header's claim that the three cutover cases were exhaustive was overstated: they are exhaustive only over draws that *take* the advisory key, which excludes a backend already inside the pre-barrier body when this migration lands. That bound is now stated in the file, and the case is closed by the tier-split migration's own preflight rather than here, since it is a property of the gap between the two applies. A mirror postflight assertion was added: the wrapper must be executable by `authenticated` after the replace, so a defensive revoke cannot silently break the quote builder's draw modal for every user. That assertion was mutation-tested — against a fixture with the grant withheld and the migration's own grant removed it aborts with `POSTFLIGHT_FAILED`, while the unmodified migration restores the grant and passes. The full file was re-applied end to end on a rebuilt PostgreSQL 17.10 fixture whose wrapper reproduced the pinned live md5 exactly, and it succeeded with its postflight notice. | 889 | 20260817120000 | **LOCAL CANDIDATE — NOT APPLIED.** File: `20260817120000_carry_allocated_line_cents_through_lifecycle.sql`, branch `claude/draw-down-price-rounding`. **Apply this one THIRD, after `20260816110000` then `20260816120000`.** It closes the blocker the adversarial review raised against that pair (CRX-MONEY-LIFECYCLE-001, High). Once the tier split emits one order line per price tier, a drawn line's `total_price` is a deliberate allocation — the tier's cumulative rounded value minus the cents already committed — so it no longer equals `ROUND(price_per_unit * quantity, 2)` and must not be recomputed. Each re-extended unit price by quantity, so every fractional segment rounded on its own: book 0.50 units at $0.50, draw it as two 0.25 lines allocated 13c and 12c, deliver both, and the customer is billed 26c against a 25c booking; cancel the remainder of a tier-compensating line and the extra cent propagates into the order header and pending commissions. This migration adds two STABLE, non-`SECURITY DEFINER` helpers — `public._allocated_cumulative_cents(uuid, numeric)` returns the integer cents owed once a given cumulative quantity has shipped, prorated from the line's stored allocation, and `public._allocated_delivery_cents(uuid, numeric, uuid)` telescopes that against cents already billed on live invoices — then routes all four sites through them. Telescoping is deliberately based on **cents actually billed**, not on recomputed units, so the result is path-independent through a void or reversal; voided and cancelled invoices are excluded, and the difference is floored at zero. `unit_price_cents` is left untouched so the below-cost trigger's fast path still fires and the customer-facing invoice PDF, which prints `extended_cents`, shows the allocated figure. A fifth recompute site inside `_enforce_below_cost_line` is deliberately **out of scope** and documented in the migration header: it fires only under an explicit admin repricing operation, where re-extending is the correct behaviour; its frontend twin is `src/pages/InvoiceDetail.tsx:696`. The migration opens with md5 preconditions on all three bodies and closes with postconditions asserting every rewritten body now calls a helper, that none still matches the three re-extension patterns, that `_allocated_cumulative_cents` is STABLE, and that neither helper is `SECURITY DEFINER` or EXECUTE-able by `anon`/`authenticated`. Receipt DETAIL follows the already-live sales-rep-reachable return lifecycle precedent from `20260812130145`, with high-entropy keys inside the shared office booking/order visibility boundary. Apply-time preflight deterministically refuses autocommit and non-READ-COMMITTED execution, takes the committed draw-cutover key exclusively, drains lock-participating legacy draws, refuses new barrier participants, then blocks while any unexpired legacy receipt remains. A cached-plan call paused before its first shared-lock statement can finish later; any resulting unbound receipt fails closed through `check_idempotency_intent` and opens its committed order rather than redrawing. **Operator consequence:** receipts live 24 hours, so the eventual apply requires a deliberate 24-hour freeze with no successful booking draws, preferably off-season or over a weekend; verify the read-only count is zero and keep draws paused through commit. Pre/postflight also refuse overload, owner, `SECURITY DEFINER`, search-path, ACL, helper, cutover-ordering, or receipt-binding drift and SHA-256-pin the exact reviewed tier-split function plus all five allocated-cent lifecycle bodies. The hash-bearing lifecycle migration, this migration, its smoke, its prover, and the already-applied migration that defines `check_idempotency_intent` are LF-pinned. A read-only live catalog read on 2026-08-20 confirmed that helper is stored LF-only, matching the reviewed body hash; the static proof now binds every hash to its exact catalog variable. The public wrapper is executable only by `authenticated`; `service_role` remains revoked because the wrapper requires `auth.uid()` and no service caller exists. All six registered smoke chains that create booking draws now provide unique per-run retry keys, so the required-key cutover does not disable the draw-ledger, order-lock, save/restore guard, or planned-hold regression lanes. The migration header records the separately approved emergency revert: drain draws under the cutover lock, drop the new wrapper, rename the preserved private wrapper back, restore prior grants, and verify body/ACL state before resuming; this deliberately restores the pre-intent stale-receipt risk and must not be approximated with `CREATE OR REPLACE`. QuoteBuilder retires permanently refused keys, opens a receipt-proven prior order, directs the operator to Orders when a changed-intent receipt cannot be opened, reports a balance reload only when every reload read succeeds, maps auth/role and malformed-product failures to governed operator language, and generation-binds initial-load cleanup. Focused migration/RPC and real component tests cover the contracts. The network-isolated PostgreSQL 17 prover applies this migration verbatim over exact prerequisites, mutation-tests stale fingerprints and prerequisite drift, proves autocommit refusal, proves the exclusive cutover lock drains and detects an in-flight legacy receipt, and refuses keyless calls. A second database restores baseline `20260727174805`, visibly reports the quarantined one-shot data migration, replays all 60 default replay-eligible ledger-selected migrations through this candidate, and executes the candidate rollback smoke plus all six registered draw/auth chains against the real money, commission, inventory, quote-version, planned-hold, and draw-ledger implementation to `SMOKE_PASS_ROLLBACK`: one exact $10 sale / $5 cost / $5 profit line/header, one exact $5 commission for the intended rep, one inventory prebooking, and one draw-ledger row; then a container-only committing proof confirms duplicate-product id-less revisions still fail closed on `QUOTE_ITEM_AMBIGUOUS_COST` and a supported two-product/two-section id-less save preserves two stamped order-line bindings through the deferred foreign-key check at `COMMIT`. SQL sha256: `8fc1665ed3a00750bf43ed01b4412c139dd2b56775a387465c99f44ce76a3199`. **No live migration apply is authorized by this repository change; live remains untouched.** |
| 891 | 20260820120000 | **LOCAL CANDIDATE — NOT APPLIED.** File: `20260820120000_save_job_enforce_chem_unit_invariant_and_derive_totals.sql`, branch `claude/save-job-server-side-chem-unit`. **Apply order relative to rows 887-890 is unconstrained** — it shares no object with the draw-down candidates; it replaces the body of `public.save_job(uuid, jsonb, jsonb, jsonb, uuid, text)` and nothing else. `CREATE OR REPLACE` on the identical six-argument signature, so no overload is added and the existing `authenticated` / `service_role` EXECUTE grants are preserved; `SECURITY DEFINER` and `SET search_path = public, pg_temp` are carried through unchanged. Closes the standing review finding that the chemical-unit and money invariants were enforced **only in React**: EXECUTE is granted to `authenticated`, so a stale tab, a replayed request or a hand-built RPC call reached the old body directly, which took `total_cost_cents` / `total_price_cents` straight from `p_job_payload` with `COALESCE` and inserted `job_chemicals` without ever comparing `unit` against `rate_unit`. **Eight changes, nothing else.** (1) A chemical line whose units provably disagree is refused with `CHEM_UNIT_MISMATCH`, naming the product and both units — skip a row with no `product_id`, skip a zero quantity, refuse a billing line whose unit is blank (change 5), skip when the two units are equal, and treat `quantity = rate x acres` carried into the price's unit as the **only** proof of safety, at a tolerance that was ORIGINALLY `GREATEST(0.0001, \|carried\| * 1e-6)` and is now a FIXED `0.0001` (round 18 removed the relative term as a money bug: a per-million slack scales with the number it checks, so at 10,000 acres it permitted a 10-unit discrepancy). Read the round-18 note below before trusting any tolerance description earlier in this row. (2) A rate measured per something other than acres is refused with `CHEM_RATE_DENOMINATOR_NOT_ACRES` in the slash form (`oz/cwt`), the spelled-out form (`oz per cwt`) and the hyphenated form (`oz-per-cwt`); the per-acre exclusion is plural-tolerant on both sides, so `gal per acres` still saves. (3) A quantity that is negative, `NaN` or `Infinity` is refused with `CHEM_QUANTITY_NOT_FINITE` **before** the unit comparison and regardless of its outcome, because it reaches the money totals whether or not the units agree. (4) `total_cost_cents` / `total_price_cents` are derived from `p_chemicals` via `safe_cents_qty` (exact `numeric`, `ROUND` half away from zero, applied per line then summed, `customer_supplied` contributing 0) and the caller-supplied totals are ignored — which is what makes a stale tab harmless. The derivation is byte-for-concept identical to what `transfer_job_to_invoice` already bills, so the stored job header and the invoice agree by construction. (5) A chemical line that actually bills but whose rate unit or stock `unit` is blank is refused with `CHEM_UNIT_UNSPECIFIED` — the round-6 policy change described below; `customer_supplied` lines, lines carrying neither a cost nor a price, and zero-quantity lines are exempt because they cannot bill wrongly. (6) The idempotency lookup routes through a canonical, advisory-locked helper instead of a raw unlocked `SELECT`, closing a pre-existing duplicate-job hole — the round-8 change described below. (7) That helper is `check_idempotency_intent(text, text, uuid, text)`, so the key is bound to the **calling actor** and to a **sha256 fingerprint of the request** (job id, job payload, fields and chemical lines, each array order-normalised before hashing) — the round-9 change described below. An unchanged retry still replays to the same job; another actor's receipt raises `IDEMPOTENCY_ACTOR_MISMATCH`; a changed payload on a spent key raises `IDEMPOTENCY_INTENT_MISMATCH` instead of returning the earlier success while saving nothing. (8) A DRY product measured or priced in fluid ounces **on either side** is refused with `CHEM_UNIT_FORM_MISMATCH` — the round-10 change described below, corrected in round 12 from an equality-gated form to an unconditional one. **Retractions, recorded because earlier versions of this row asserted the opposite.** (a) This is a real behaviour change, **not** a mirror of a shipped client guard: `chemLineBillingHazard`, `rateDenominatorIsUnrecognized` and `centsTimesQuantity` exist only on the **unmerged** PR #436 branch, `main` has no save-blocking unit guard at all, and `reconcileChemAutofillUnits` has a documented safe-fallback path that actively manufactures the refused shape. **ORDERING PREREQUISITE: land PR #436 before applying**, or the first operator to touch an affected job gets a hard save failure with no prior on-screen warning, and because `performSave` re-sends the whole chemical grid one bad legacy line makes the entire job unsaveable. (b) The page and this function do **not** agree to the cent: `main` computes displayed totals with IEEE-754 binary multiply and half-up rounding, so the operator can see a figure one cent off what is stored until PR #436 lands the exact-cents client math. (c) An earlier version of this work split the preflight body pin into its own migration and justified the split by a claimed lexer defect in the `idempotency-body-check` guard. **Both are withdrawn.** The guard has no such defect — it lexes the edit fragment, and the fragment under test had ended mid-signature on an unclosed parenthesis, so the guard was correctly reporting an unbalanced parameter list in what it was given. The pin is now an in-file `DO $preflight$` block immediately preceding the `CREATE OR REPLACE`, which is the point: Codex and the drift reviewer independently found that two separately ledgered migrations are not atomic, so a committed pin plus a failed replacement left the next run free to overwrite an unvalidated body. The pin asserts the RPC exists, has exactly one overload, and that its installed body is md5 `7e1668161ae287086e76a8ba5bd313d6` (installed by migration 20260706080000, read live 2026-08-23; the live text carries no CR bytes, so the md5 is over exactly what is stored and line endings must **not** be normalised before comparing). Its second arm keys a replay no-op on the body marker `chem_unit_invariant_v1` rather than on the operator-visible error text, so a later revision that bumps the marker is correctly refused; a later migration copying the marker verbatim would still pass, so any body change must bump it. An in-file postflight then asserts one overload, `prosecdef`, `search_path = public, pg_temp`, EXECUTE held by `authenticated` and `service_role`, and not held by `anon` or `PUBLIC`. **Proof** (`scripts/smoke/prove-save-job-chem-unit-invariant.mjs`, PostgreSQL 17 matching production 17.6, re-runnable, committed): the pinned md5 reproduces from migration 20260706080000; against a different body the migration aborts with `PREFLIGHT_BODY_DRIFT` and leaves the installed body unchanged by the same md5 **and the same exact byte length**; a replay reinstalls the identical body (it does not skip — the marker only suppresses the drift error, and the prover fingerprints the function either side of the replay and requires equality); the apply corrects a deliberately bad starting ACL; 58 behaviour tests pass; and 31 mutation phases each fail in a **named** way — 25 turn a named behaviour test red, and 6 must abort the apply with the specific postflight assertion that catches them (`POSTFLIGHT_NOT_SECURITY_DEFINER`, `POSTFLIGHT_ANON_EXECUTE`, `POSTFLIGHT_PUBLIC_EXECUTE`, `POSTFLIGHT_GRANT_LOST`, `POSTFLIGHT_SEARCH_PATH`), which review found were previously never exercised because the harness pre-installed a correct ACL. **Known residual, stated not hidden:** `job_fields.acres_to_treat` still carries no CHECK, so a `NaN` acreage can no longer bypass the invariant but can still be stored. Blast radius re-verified read-only 2026-08-23 against every `job_chemicals` row: 4 rows, none negative, none non-finite, derived totals reproduce every stored total to the cent. **Round-4 review changes.** (i) The file now **establishes** the ACL it asserts — `REVOKE EXECUTE … FROM PUBLIC`, `FROM anon`, and `GRANT … TO authenticated, service_role` run before the postflight. On live these are a no-op (read read-only 2026-08-23: `postgres=X/postgres | authenticated=X/postgres | service_role=X/postgres`, `anon` holding nothing, exactly one 6-argument overload and no other), but no migration in this repository has ever revoked this function from `anon` or `PUBLIC` and the only grant it ever received was `TO authenticated` (migration 20260624120000) — so a database replayed from migrations alone would have carried an inherited `anon` grant on a `SECURITY DEFINER` write path, with only an assertion standing against it. Prover phase 4 now starts from that deliberately bad ACL and proves the migration corrects it. (ii) The per-acre exclusion and the suffix stripper are now character-identical and accept the same abbreviations as the slash form (`acres|acre|ac|a`); the third asymmetry found in review made `pt per ac` a **false refusal**, which blocks an entire job rather than one line. (iii) The per-acre stripper is deliberately **wider** than `normalize_rate_unit`, which strips only `\s+per\s+acre$`. That divergence is kept on purpose and stated in the header rather than papered over: narrowing it does not remove the disagreement, it converts it into a false refusal, and the divergence runs in the permissive direction and cannot mis-bill because nothing downstream multiplies money by `rate_unit` (billing and inventory both read `job_chemicals.unit`). Aligning `normalize_rate_unit` and the client `baseUnitOfRate` to the wider form is the correct follow-up and is deliberately not bundled into a money migration. (iv) The catalog lookups now spell the function name as a plain literal; an earlier draft built it by concatenation, which defeated a `grep` for the very assertions that pin it. (v) **APPLY CHANNEL: Supabase MCP `apply_migration` only** — the file is seven top-level statements (`DO $preflight$`, the `CREATE OR REPLACE`, two `REVOKE`s, a `GRANT`, `DO $postflight$`, `COMMENT ON FUNCTION`) and `execute_sql` returns only the last, so through that channel the pin, the replacement, the ACL correction and the postflight would all be silently skipped. **Round-5 review changes.** The finiteness check moved AHEAD of the missing-product skip — the derived-totals `SELECT` sums every element of `p_chemicals`, including rows the loop skips, so a row with no `product_id` and a `NaN` quantity bypassed the check and then aborted inside `safe_cents_qty` with a raw `cannot convert NaN to bigint` instead of the operator-facing refusal. The `COMMENT ON FUNCTION` no longer implies PR #436 warns about everything this refuses: that PR tests for a slash, so it flags neither the spelled-out nor the hyphenated denominator, and it has no counterpart at all for the non-finite-quantity refusal. The registered smoke chain's two sentinels moved outside the blocks whose own `EXCEPTION WHEN OTHERS` was catching them, which had made a genuine miss report as "raised wrong error". And `scripts/smoke/smoke-specs.json` now states plainly that the container prover is **manual** — `run-smoke.mjs` executes a `container_prover` only on the container-only skip path, so for a spec with a live SQL chain it merely checks the file exists and `--all` never runs it. **Round-6, and it is a POLICY change Mason settled on 2026-08-23, not a review fix.** A chemical line that BILLS but whose rate unit or stock `unit` is BLANK is now REFUSED with `CHEM_UNIT_UNSPECIFIED`. It used to be skipped — a blank unit proves nothing — but `transfer_job_to_invoice` bills such a line anyway, which makes an unprovable billable line the same hazard class as a provably wrong one, and it was the largest hole left open. Exempt, deliberately: `customer_supplied` lines and lines carrying neither a cost nor a price, because a line that cannot bill cannot bill wrongly; the test covers the cost side as well as the price, since `total_cost_cents` feeds margin. **PRE-APPLY DATA OBLIGATION — SATISFIED 2026-08-24, not retired.** Exactly one live `job_chemicals` row was in the refused shape (JOB-2026-0002: a `pt/ac` rate, blank unit, both a cost and a price, not customer-supplied). Mason chose to correct the data FIRST so the guard lands with zero operational impact, and that correction was made on 2026-08-24 with his explicit OK — one row, `unit` set to `'Pt'`.
